- `[1]` **[SF]** She was the second wife of Colonel William Byrd III, a Colonial American military officer at the time of the American Revolution and son of the founder of Richmond, Virginia.
- `[2]` Her father, Charles Willing, was the mayor of Philadelphia from 1748 to 1754, and her great-grandfather, Edward Shippen, was the second mayor of Philadelphia, from 1701 to 1703.
- `[3]` Her husband committed suicide in January 1777, leaving considerable debts.
- `[4]` She managed his plantations, including Westover Plantation, in Charles City County, in order to satisfy his creditors and still preserve some property for their ten children to inherit.
- `[5]` She sold off some western lands, residences in Richmond and Williamsburg, and other property, but she was able to retain control of Westover, the major Byrd plantation in Charles City County.
- `[6]` Although Byrd had many ties to the British and Loyalists during the American Revolution, she tried to remain neutral.
- `[7]` After trying to recover property that had been seized by the British, she was charged in 1781 by the state of Virginia with trading with the enemy.
- `[8]` Byrd defended herself eloquently in a letter to Governor Thomas Jefferson: "I wish well to all mankind, to America in particular.
- `[9]` What am I but an American?
- `[10]` All my friends and connexions are in America; my whole property is here—
- `[11]` could I wish ill to everything I have an interest in?"
- `[12]` Her trial was first postponed and ultimately never held.
- `[13]` In 2007 Byrd was posthumously honored by the Library of Virginia's "Virginia Women in History" program.

- `[4]` William Byrd III had a reputation as a notorious gambler.

- `[6]` Byrd eventually fathered five children by his first wife (Eliza Carter, m. 1748, d. 1760), and fathered ten more by his second wife, Mary Willing, daughter of Charles Willing of Philadelphia.

- `[9]` Although his sale of land and slaves generated a huge sum it still was not enough to pay off his creditors.
- `[10]` Later, Byrd resorted to a lottery, the prizes of which would come from his estate, Belvidere, at the falls of the James River.
- `[11]` However the lottery failed to generate sufficient revenue.
- `[12]` Despondent and nearly broke, Byrd committed suicide on January 1 or 2, 1777.
- `[13]` He was buried in the cemetery at the old Westover Church.
- `[14]` The 10 children of his second marriage (to Mary Willing) were:

- `[2]` The film, set in the early 1950s, is based on Donn Pearce's 1965 novel of the same name.
- `[3]` Pearce sold the story to Warner Bros., who then hired him to write the script.
- `[4]` Due to Pearce's lack of film experience, the studio added Frank Pierson to rework the screenplay.
- `[5]` Newman's biographer Marie Edelman Borden states that the" tough, honest" script drew together threads from earlier movies, especially" Hombre", Newman's earlier film of 1967.
- `[6]` The film has been cited by Roger Ebert as an anti-establishment film which was shot during the time of emerging popular opposition to the Vietnam War.
- `[7]` Newman's character, Lucas Jackson, is described( by the notorious" Captain" upon his arrival at the prison), as a" free spirit," whose personal record( read out loud because of its unusual details) indicates a man who started well in the U.S. Army—receiving medals for bravery in" the war" —rose to the rank of sergeant, yet was discharged as a" buck" private.
- `[8]` Luke does not question his physical incarceration, and initially has no thought of escape.
- `[9]` But his spirit is not, like that of his fellow inmates, imprisoned.
- `[10]` This free thinking is, from the outset, noticed by the institution, its functionaries( the guards), and especially its leaders.
- `[11]` Their response is a mixture of both fear and loathing.
- `[12]` So they retaliate against Luke through" physical punishment, psychological cruelty, hopelessness and equal parts of sadism and masochism."
- `[13]` His influence on his prison mates and the torture that he endures is compared to that of Jesus, and Christian symbolism is used throughout the film, culminating in a photograph superimposed over crossroads at the end of the film in comparison to the crucifixion.
- `[14]` Filming took place within California's San Joaquin River Delta region; the set, imitating a prison farm in the Deep South, was based on photographs and measurements made by a crew sent to Road Prison in Gainesville, Florida by the filmmakers.
- `[15]` Upon its release," Cool Hand Luke" received favorable reviews and became a box- office success.
- `[16]` The film cemented Newman's status as one of the era's top box- office actors, while the film was described as the" touchstone of an era." Newman was nominated for the Academy Award for Best Actor, George Kennedy won the Academy Award for Best Supporting Actor, Pearce and Pierson were nominated for the Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay, and the score by Lalo Schifrin was also nominated for the Best Original Score.
- `[17]` In 2005, the United States Library of Congress selected it for the National Film Registry, considering it to be" culturally, historically, or aesthetically significant."
- `[18]` It has a 100% rating on the review aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes.
- `[19]` The quotation used by the prison warden( Strother Martin) in the film, which begins with" What we've got here is failure to communicate," was listed at No. 11 on the American Film Institute's list of the 100 most memorable movie lines.

### Antonio José de Irisarri

- `[0]` **[SF]** Antonio José de Irisarri Alonso (February 7, 1786 – June 10, 1868), was a Guatemalan statesman, journalist, and politician who served as Interim Supreme Director of Chile in 1814.
- `[1]` He is considered one of the fathers of Chilean journalism together with Fr. Camilo Henríquez.
- `[2]` Irisarri was born in Guatemala City, the son of Juan Bautista de Irisarri and of María de la Paz Alonso.
- `[3]` He studied in his native city and in Europe, from whence he was recalled back to his home after the death of his father in 1805.
- `[4]` In 1809 he visited Chile, and having married Mercedes Trucíos y Larraín, an heiress there, took up his residence in that country and joined with enthusiasm the movement for independence in 1810.
- `[5]` He had charge of important public offices during the struggle for liberty, including the command of the National guard and the civil and military government of the province of Santiago, and from March 7 to 14, 1814, he was temporarily in charge of the supreme direction of the nation.
- `[6]` In 1818 he was appointed Minister of Government and Foreign Affairs by Bernardo O'Higgins, and in October of the same year he went to Buenos Aires as minister.
- `[7]` At the end of 1819 he was sent to Europe to negotiate the recognition of Chilean Independence by England and France (which he was not able to obtain.)
- `[8]` While at London he negotiated a loan of £1,000,000 (the first foreign debt of Chile.)
- `[9]` He was sent to Central America in 1827 as minister for Chile, and in 1837 as plenipotentiary to Peru and private adviser to Admiral Manuel Blanco during the War of the Confederation.
- `[10]` After the Chilean army was forced to sign the Treaty of Paucarpata on November 17, 1837, the scandal was gigantic.
- `[11]` Irisarri refused to return to Chile, was tried "in absentia" for high treason, and sentence to death.
- `[12]` Irisarri wisely never returned to Chile.
- `[13]` He was minister of Guatemala and Salvador to Ecuador from 1839 till 1845, and in 1846-1848 to Colombia, but resigned, and went to Curaçao in 1849, and in 1850 to the United States, where he resided till his death.
- `[14]` The governments of Guatemala and Salvador appointed him, in 1855, their minister to Washington, and for a long time he was dean of the diplomatic corps.
- `[15]` Irisarri continued his literary work in the United States, and was generally esteemed for his knowledge, genial character, and polished manners.
- `[16]` Irisarri was chief editor of the "Semanario Republicano de Chile" in Santiago in 1813; of "El Duende" in the same city in 1818; of "El Censor Americano" in London in 1820; of "El Guatemalteco" in Guatemala in 1828; of "La Verdad desnuda,La Balanza," and "El Correo" in Guayaquil in 1839-1843; of "La Concordia" in Quito in 1844-1845; of" Nosotros,Orden y Libertad," and "El Cristiano Errante" in Bogota in 1846-1847; and of "El Revisor" in Curaçao in 1849, the publication of which he continued in New York.
- `[17]` He also published "La defensa de la historia critica del asesinato cometido en la persona del Gran Mariseal de Ayacucho" (Quito, 1845); "Memoria biografica del Arzobispo Mosquera" (Bogota, 1848); a collection of his satirical poems, a novel, "Cuestiones Filologicas," and several pamphlets.
- `[18]` He died in Brooklyn, New York, on June 10, 1868.
- `[19]` In 1973 Guatemala honoured him with the institution of an "Order of Antonio José de Irisarri".

- `[0]` Sergei Parajanov (sometimes spelled Paradzhanov or Paradjanov; January 9, 1924 – July 20, 1990) was a Soviet film director and artist of Armenian descent who made significant contributions to Soviet cinematography through Ukrainian, Georgian, and Armenian cinema.
- `[1]` He invented his own cinematic style, which was totally out of step with the guiding principles of socialist realism (the only sanctioned art style in the USSR).
- `[2]` This, combined with his controversial lifestyle and behaviour, led Soviet authorities to repeatedly persecute and imprison him, and suppress his films.
- `[3]` Although he started professional film-making in 1954, Parajanov later disowned all the films he made before 1965 as "garbage".
- `[4]` After directing "Shadows of Forgotten Ancestors" (renamed "Wild Horses of Fire" for most foreign distributions)
- `[5]` Parajanov became something of an international celebrity and simultaneously a target of attacks from the system.
- `[6]` Nearly all of his film projects and plans from 1965 to 1973 were banned, scrapped or closed by the Soviet film administrations, both local (in Kiev and Yerevan) and federal (Goskino), almost without discussion, until he was finally arrested in late 1973 on charges of rape, homosexuality and bribery.
- `[7]` He was imprisoned until 1977, despite a pleas for pardon from various artists.
- `[8]` Even after his release (he was arrested for the third and last time in 1982)
- `[9]` he was a "persona non grata" in Soviet cinema.
- `[10]` It was not until the mid-1980s, when the political climate started to relax, that he could resume directing.
- `[11]` Still, it required the help of influential Georgian actor Dodo Abashidze and other friends to have his last feature films greenlighted.
- `[12]` His health seriously weakened by four years in labor camps and nine months in prison in Tbilisi, Parajanov died of lung cancer in 1990, at a time when, after almost 20 years of suppression, his films were being featured at foreign film festivals.
- `[13]` In January 1988, he said in an interview, "Everyone knows that I have three Motherlands.
- `[14]` I was born in Georgia, worked in Ukraine
- `[15]` and I'm going to die in Armenia."
- `[16]` **[SF]** Sergei Parajanov is buried at Komitas Pantheon in Yerevan.

- `[7]` He was responsible for the construction of the famous "Hortus Palatinus" gardens in Heidelberg.
- `[8]` In 1618 the largely Protestant estates of Bohemia rebelled against their Catholic King Ferdinand, triggering the outbreak of the Thirty Years' War.
- `[9]` Frederick was asked to assume the crown of Bohemia.
- `[10]` He accepted the offer and was crowned on 4 November 1619, as Frederick I (the adjective means "of Oberpfalz" or "of the Upper Palatinate").
- `[11]` The estates chose Frederick since he was the leader of the Protestant Union, a military alliance founded by his father, and hoped for the support of Frederick's father-in-law, James VI of Scotland and I of England.
- `[12]` However, James opposed the takeover of Bohemia from the Habsburgs and Frederick's allies in the Protestant Union failed to support him militarily by signing the Treaty of Ulm (1620).
- `[13]` His brief reign as King of Bohemia ended with his defeat at the Battle of White Mountain on 8 November 1620 – a year and four days after his coronation.
- `[14]` After the battle, the Imperial forces invaded Frederick's Palatine lands and he had to flee to his uncle Prince Maurice, Stadtholder of the Dutch Republic in 1622.
- `[15]` An Imperial edict formally deprived him of the Palatinate in 1623.
- `[16]` He lived the rest of his life in exile with his wife and family, mostly at The Hague, and died in Mainz in 1632.
- `[17]` His eldest surviving son Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, returned to power in 1648 with the end of the war.
- `[18]` Another son was Prince Rupert of the Rhine, one of the most colourful figures of his time.
- `[19]` His daughter Princess Sophia was eventually named heiress presumptive to the British throne, and is the founder of the Hanoverian line of kings.

### Huchtenburg

- `[0]` J( oh) an and Jacob van Huchtenburg( Hughtenburg or Hugtenburg( h)) were two Dutch Golden Age painters in the second half of the seventeenth century.
- `[1]` Both brothers were natives of Haarlem, moved to Paris, but died in Amsterdam.

- `[4]` Jacob van Huchtenburg( 1644, Haarlem- bur. January 8, 1675, Amsterdam) studied under Nicolaes Pieterszoon Berchem.
- `[5]` In 1662 he went to Italy and stayed in Rome until 1667.

- `[7]` In 1669 he joined the Haarlem artists' guild.
- `[8]` His pictures are probably confounded with those of his brother.

- `[10]` One is in the Brukenthal Museum, Sibiu.
- `[11]` **[SF]** Jan van Huchtenburg( bapt. November 20, 1647, Haarlem- bur. July 2, 1733, Amsterdam), was a famous Dutch horse and battle painter, like Esaias van de Velde and Philip Wouwermans.
- `[12]` He was first taught by Thomas Wijck.
- `[13]` On his way to visit his brother in Rome, he may not have got further than Paris, where he served under Antony Francis van der Meulen in the Manufacture des Gobelins employing him for illustrating, sketching or designing.
- `[14]` In 1670 he settled at Haarlem, where he married Elisabeth Mommes.
- `[15]` It seems he practised and kept a dealers shop in Haarlem or in the Hague.
- `[16]` His style had now merged into an imitation of Wouwerman and Van der Meulen, which could not fail to produce pretty pictures of hunts and robber camps, the faculty of painting horses and men in action and varied dress being the chief point of attraction.
- `[17]` Huchtenburg assisted Gerrit Berckheyde and painted his people and horses.
- `[18]` Later Huchtenburg ventured on cavalry skirmishes and engagements of regular troops generally, and these were admired by Prince Eugene of Savoy and King William III, who gave the painter sittings, and commissioned him to throw upon canvas the chief incidents of the battles they fought upon the continent of Europe.
- `[19]` When he died at Bloemgracht in the Jordaan in 1733, Huchtenburg had done much by his pictures and prints to make Prince Eugene, King William and Marlborough popular.
- `[20]` Though clever in depicting a mile or a skirmish of dragoons, he remained second to Philip Wouvermans in accuracy of drawing, and inferior to Van der Meulen in the production of landscapes.
- `[21]` But, nevertheless, he was a clever and spirited master, with great facility of hand and considerable natural powers of observation.
- `[22]` The earliest date on his pictures is 1674, when he executed the Stag- Hunt in the Museum of Berlin, and the Fight with Robbers in the Liechtenstein Museum at Vienna.
- `[23]` A Skirmish at Fleurus( 1690) in the Brussels gallery seems but the precursor of larger and more powerful works, such as the Siege of Namur( 1695) in the Belvedere at Vienna, where William III is seen in the foreground accompanied by Max Emmanuel, the Bavarian elector.
- `[24]` Three years before, Huchtenburg had had sittings from Prince Eugene and William III.
- `[25]` After 1696 he regularly served as court painter to Prince Eugene, and we have at[ a] Galleria Sabauda a series of ten or eleven canvases, all of the same size depicting the various battles of the great hero, commencing with the Battle of Zenta( 1697), Battle of Chiari( 1701), Battle of Luzzara( 1702), Battle of Blenheim( 1704), Battle of Cassano( 1705), Battle of Turin( 1706), Battle of Oudenaarde( 1708), Battle of Malplaquet( 1709), Battle of Petrovaradin( 1716) ending with the Battle of Belgrade( 1717).
- `[26]` Had the Duke of Marlborough been fond of art he would doubtless have possessed many works of our artist.
- `[27]` All that remained in 1911 at Blenheim Palace, however, was a couple of sketches of battles, which were probably sent to Churchill by his great contemporary.
- `[28]` In 1911, the pictures of Huchtenburg were not very numerous in public galleries.
- `[29]` There was one in the National Gallery, London, another at the Louvre.
- `[30]` But Copenhagen had four, Dresden six, Gotha two, and Munich had the well- known composition of Tallart taken Prisoner at Blenheim in 1704.

- `[7]` Of his Beatles songs, more than 2,200 artists have covered" Yesterday"( 1965), making it one of the most covered songs in popular music history.
- `[8]` In 1970, McCartney debuted as a solo artist with the album" McCartney".
- `[9]` Throughout the 1970s, he led Wings, one of the most successful bands of the decade, with more than a dozen international top 10 singles and albums.
- `[10]` McCartney resumed his solo career in 1980.
- `[11]` Since 1989, he has toured consistently as a solo artist, and in 1993, he formed the music duo the Fireman with Youth of Killing Joke.

- `[13]` McCartney is one of the most successful composers and performers of all time.

### Chlothar III

- `[0]` **[SF]** Chlothar III (or "ChlotarClotharClotaireChlotochar", or "Hlothar", giving rise to the name Lothair; 652–73) was the eldest son of Clovis II, king of Neustria and Burgundy, and his queen Balthild.
- `[1]` When Clovis died in 658, Chlothar succeeded him under the regency of his mother.
- `[2]` Only a month beforehand, according to the near-contemporary "Life of Eligius" by the courtier Audoin (bishop) of Rouen, Saint Eligius had prophesied the death of Clovis, Balthild's downfall, and Chlothar's short reign.
- `[3]` Few things are known about the time of Chlothar's reign.
- `[4]` The "Historia Langobardorum" reports that in the early 660s a Frankish army invaded Provence and then Italy.
- `[5]` This force came upon the camp of the Lombard king Grimoald I of Benevento, at Rivoli near Asta.
- `[6]` Grimuald pretended to flee.
- `[7]` The Franks looted the camp and celebrated.
- `[8]` Then, after midnight, Grimuald attacked and drove them back to Neustria.
- `[9]` After the death of Saint Eligius in 661, the "Life of Eligius" records that a plague reduced the population of France's cities.
- `[10]` A plague in the British Isles, according to Bede, did the same there in 664.
- `[11]` During the regency, Austrasians requested a king of their own and, in 662, Chlothar's court sent another son of Clovis II, Childeric II, to be king there.
- `[12]` Also during his reign, the mayor of the palace Erchinoald died and a council of Franks elected Ebroin to replace him.
- `[13]` Ebroin's early administrative authority was significant: Bede tells the story of how, in 668, the newly appointed Theodore of Canterbury could only travel through the Frankish kingdoms from Rome with the mayor's permission.
- `[14]` Chlothar may have been more politically active after this time, as he reached the age of majority in 669.
- `[15]` The nearest contemporary chronicle, the Liber Historiae Francorum of 727, relates only that he ruled for four years (presumably a reference to his active years 669-673) and then died.
- `[16]` He is confirmed as still being in the sixteenth year of his reign in a chronological note in a Victorian Easter table of 673.
- `[17]` His brother Theuderic III succeeded him as king later that same year.
- `[18]` It is notable that he is often described as the first "roi fainéant"—do-nothing king—of the Merovingian dynasty.

- Nelson A. Rockefeller | place of death | New York

**Context:**

### Place of birth

- `[0]` The place of birth( POB) or birthplace is the place where a person was born.
- `[1]` This place is often used in legal documents, together with name and date of birth, to uniquely identify a person.
- `[2]` As a general rule with respect to passports, the place of birth is determined to be the country that currently has" sovereignty" over the actual place of birth, regardless of when the birth actually occurred.
- `[3]` The place of birth is not necessarily the place where the parents of the new baby live.
- `[4]` If the baby is born in a hospital in another place, that place is the place of birth.
- `[5]` In many countries, this also means that the government requires that the birth of the new baby is registered in the place of birth.
- `[6]` Some countries place less or no importance on the place of birth, instead using alternative geographical characteristics for the purpose of identity documents.
- `[7]` For example, Sweden has used the concept of" födelsehemortdomicile of birth") since 1947.
- `[8]` This means that the domicile of the baby's mother is the registered place of birth.
- `[9]` The location of the maternity ward or other physical birthplace is considered unimportant.
- `[10]` Similarly, Switzerland uses the concept of" Heimatortlieu d'origineluogo d'origineplace of origin" in German, French and Italian respectively).
- `[11]` A child born to Swiss parents is automatically assigned the place of origin of the parent with the same last name, so the child either gets their mother's or father's place of origin.
- `[12]` A child born to one Swiss parent and one foreign parent acquires the place of origin of their Swiss parent.
- `[13]` In a Swiss passport and identity card, the holder's place of origin is stated, not their place of birth.
- `[14]` In some countries( primarily in the Americas), the place of birth automatically determines the nationality of the baby, a practice often referred to by the Latin phrase" jus soli".
- `[15]` Almost all countries outside the Americas instead attribute nationality based on the nationality( ies) of the baby's parents( referred to as" jus sanguinis").
- `[16]` There can be some confusion regarding the place of birth if the birth takes place in an unusual way: when babies are born on an airplane or at sea, difficulties can arise.
- `[17]` The place of birth of such a person depends on the law of the countries involved, which include the nationality of the plane or ship, the nationality( ies) of the parents and/ or the location of the plane or ship( if the birth occurs in the territorial waters or airspace of a country).
- `[18]` Some administrative forms may request the applicant's" country of birth".
- `[19]` It is important to determine from the requester whether the information requested refers to the applicant's" place of birth" or" nationality at birth".
- `[20]` For US citizens born abroad who under the US Constitution acquire US citizenship at the time of birth, the nationality at birth will be USA( American), while the place of birth would be the country in which the actual birth takes place.

- `[6]` As Governor of New York from 1959 to 1973, Rockefeller's achievements included the expansion of the State University of New York, efforts to protect the environment, the construction of the Governor Nelson A. Rockefeller Empire State Plaza in Albany, increased facilities and personnel for medical care, and the creation of the New York State Council on the Arts.
- `[7]` After unsuccessfully seeking the Republican presidential nomination in 1960, 1964 and 1968, Rockefeller was appointed vice president of the United States under President Gerald R. Ford, who ascended to the presidency following the August 1974 resignation of Richard Nixon.
- `[8]` Rockefeller was the second vice president appointed to the position under the 25th Amendment, following Ford himself.

- `[10]` He retired from politics in 1977 and died two years later.

- `[12]` Rockefeller assembled a significant art collection and promoted public access to the arts.
- `[13]` He served as trustee, treasurer, and president of the Museum of Modern Art, and founded the Museum of Primitive Art in 1954.
- `[14]` In the area of philanthropy, he founded the Rockefeller Brothers Fund in 1940 with his four brothers and established the American International Association for Economic and Social Development in 1946.

### Tunisian Victory

- `[0]` **[SF]** Tunisian Victory is a 1944 Anglo- American propaganda film about the victories in the North Africa Campaign.
- `[1]` The film follows both armies from the planning of Operation" Torch" and Operation" Acrobat"( the latter of which was canceled), to the liberation of Tunis.
- `[2]` Interspersed in the documentary format are the narrative voices of supposed American and British soldiers( voiced by Burgess Meredith and Bernard Miles respectively), recounting their experience in the campaign.
- `[3]` Miles and Meredith, playing the roles of soldiers, talk separately until the end of the film when they have a dialogue, agree to co-operate after the end of the war, and with the other Allied nations create a more just and peaceful post-war order.
- `[4]` The film was intended as a follow- up to the successful British documentary film" Desert Victory"( 1943).
- `[5]` Frederic Krome's article" Tunisian Victory" and Anglo- American Film Propaganda in World War II from" The Historian" details the acrimony between the British and US film makers on the project.
- `[6]` Most of the actual American combat footage taken during Operation Torch was destroyed when the ship carrying it was sunk, requiring many" battle scenes" to be reshot in the U.S. by director John Huston.
- `[7]` Huston restaged several battles and liberations to achieve high quality footage, even going so far as to film some air battle scenes( in the Mohave Desert) and in Orlando, Florida.
- `[8]` The British recognized the dubious nature of the film, though they themselves were guilty of the same recreations in wartime propaganda films.
- `[9]` The direction of the final version involved no less than five individuals: Frank Capra, John Huston, Anthony Veiller, Hugh Stewart and Roy Boulting.

### Collingwood Children's Farm

- `[0]` Collingwood Children's Farm is a not- for-profit, inner city farm situated on the Yarra River in the Melbourne suburb of Abbotsford, Australia.
- `[1]` It is adjacent to, and considered part of the larger Abbotsford Convent complex.
- `[2]` The Abbotsford Heritage Precinct Farmlands( APHF) supporting the Collingwood Children's Farm are unique in being the oldest continually farmed land in the state of Victoria.
- `[3]` Farming commenced in 1836, with formal landsales occurring in 1838.
- `[4]` Farming on this land has continued for over 175 years.
- `[5]` It is also the oldest Children's Farm in Australia, established in 1979.
- `[6]` The farm is open to the public all year round, from 9.15 am to 4:00 pm and visitors may explore the grounds at their own leisure.
- `[7]` The first Sunday of every month( excluding January) is a Family Day with a barbecue, plus horse and hay rides for the children.
- `[8]` The farm also hosts a popular monthly Farmers' market, on the second Saturday of each month; selling fresh produce from the farm and over 60+ Victorian producers.
- `[9]` To celebrate the Winter Solstice and as a fundraiser, the farm also holds an annual bonfire event.
- `[10]` The farm boasts an extensive range of animals from earthworms and peacocks to goats, sheep, horses, ducks, pigs, chickens and guinea pigs.
- `[11]` Nearly all of the animals at the farm are classified as rare breeds.
- `[12]` The horses at the farm are all fine specimens, with there currently being 8 horses( but not at the same time).
- `[13]` All the horses at the farm are ridden by staff, Young Farmers, and the Holiday Program which is run in all term holidays except summer holidays.
- `[14]` The farm began in 1979, when a community committee leased a small plot of Crown land next to the Yarra River, used by the farm and Community Gardeners.
- `[15]` Funding has come from various sources over the years and now entrance fees and donations make up the bulk of the farm's income.
- `[16]` The farm relies immensely on community effort with staff, volunteers and a Young Farmer's Programme working collaboratively to maintain the farm.
- `[17]` Visitors are able to try their hand at cow- brushing, which occurs daily at 10.15 am and 4 pm.
- `[18]` Produce from the animals, such as honey, earthworm castings, and eggs are for sale from the farm when available.
- `[19]` The farm also offers hosted birthday parties( limited to two per day) on weekends( except those days which fall on Family Day, Farmers Market Day and other special events).
- `[20]` Weddings and special events are also held at the farm.
- `[21]` Cyclists and pedestrians will often take a journey along the Yarra River Trail as it passes through the Collingwood Children's Farm, following the Yarra River from the city to Dights Falls and further afield.
- `[22]` They often stop and eat at the Farm Cafe( which is run independently of the farm).

### WD Scott

- `[1]` The company specialized in business process improvement and performance improvement.
- `[2]` In 1966, Sir Walter Scott was appointed as the Chair of the Decimal Currency Board by the Prime Minister, and WDScott( now one word without a space) was appointed to oversee the implementation of decimal currency in Australia.
- `[3]` At one point WDScott existed in many countries and regions around the globe, including Australia, New Zealand, Europe, Asia, the United Kingdom, and the United States.
- `[4]` In 2007 WDScott re-launched the brand under WDScott Ltd, a public unlisted Australian corporation, with a new international board and a management team to lead the firm.
- `[5]` The WDScott methodology and knowledge suite were re-vitalised and re-established within international academic and business communities.
- `[6]` The firm acquired Australian management consultancy Clear Lead Pty Ltd in December 2007, and subsequently made several other mergers and acquisitions, including Global Justice Solutions.
- `[7]` In 2010, WDScott merged with Distinct Consulting, an Ireland- based firm with expertise in the design and implementation of business intelligence and analytics- based solutions.
- `[8]` The firm expanded to Australia, Europe, and the US.
- `[9]` In 2013, WDScott's northern hemisphere business was acquired by multi-national firm FTI Consulting.
- `[10]` FTI also bought the rights to the Distinct brand.
- `[11]` The WDScott brand was retained by the Australian business.
- `[12]` Today, WDScott operates as an Australian Pty Ltd company with offices in Sydney, Melbourne, Brisbane, and Canberra.

### Walter D. Scott

- `[1]` Sir Walter was knighted in 1966 and appointed a Companion of the Order of Australia in 1979.
- `[2]` In the 1950s, 1960s, and 1970s, WD Scott was Australia's leading management consultancy firm.

- `[1]` The company was established primarily to supply steel ingots for Roach's steel mills, which included the Chester Rolling Mill and the Combination Steel and Iron Company, although it also manufactured steel castings.

### Philip II of Spain

- `[0]` Philip II of Spain (21 May 1527 – 13 September 1598) was King of Spain (1556–98), King of Portugal
- `[1]` (1581–98, as Philip I), King of Naples and Sicily (both from 1554), and "jure uxoris" King of England and Ireland (during his marriage to Queen Mary I from 1554 to 1558).
- `[2]` He was also Duke of Milan, and from 1555, lord of the Seventeen Provinces of the Netherlands.
- `[3]` **[SF]** The son of Holy Roman Emperor and King of the Spanish kingdoms Charles V and Isabella of Portugal, Philip was called "Felipe el PrudentePhilip the Prudent") in the Spanish kingdoms; his empire included territories on every continent then known to Europeans, including his namesake the Philippines.
- `[4]` During his reign, the Spanish kingdoms reached the height of its influence and power.
- `[5]` This is sometimes called the "Spanish Golden Age".
- `[6]` Philip led a highly debt-leveraged regime, seeing state bankruptcies in 1557, 1560, 1569, 1575, and 1596.
- `[7]` This policy was partly the cause of the declaration of independence that created the Dutch Republic in 1581.
- `[8]` On 31 December 1584 Philip signed the Treaty of Joinville, with Henry I, Duke of Guise signing on behalf of the Catholic League; consequently Philip supplied a considerable annual grant to the League over the following decade to maintain the civil war in France, with the hope of destroying the French Calvinists.
- `[9]` A devout Catholic, Philip saw himself as the defender of Catholic Europe against the Ottoman Empire and the Protestant Reformation.
- `[10]` He sent a large armada to invade Protestant England in 1588, with the strategic aim of overthrowing Elizabeth I of England and re-establishing Catholicism there.
- `[11]` He hoped to stop both English interference in the Spanish Netherlands and the harm caused to Spanish interests by English and Dutch privateering.
- `[12]` Philip was described by the Venetian ambassador Paolo Fagolo in 1563 as "slight of stature and round-faced, with pale blue eyes, somewhat prominent lip, and pink skin, but his overall appearance is very attractive".
- `[13]` The Ambassador went on to say "He dresses very tastefully, and everything that he does is courteous and gracious."
- `[14]` Besides Mary I, Philip was married three other times and widowed four times.

## 87. Where was the place of death of Gilbert Chandler's father?

**Context:**

### Alfred Chandler (politician)

- `[0]` Alfred Elliott Chandler (1 July 1873 – 12 February 1935) was an Australian politician.
- `[1]` He was born in Malvern to market gardener William Chandler and Kate Timewell.
- `[2]` **[SF]** He attended state school and became a horticulturist, running a nursery in Boronia.
- `[3]` On 24 May 1897 he married Elizabeth Ann Intermann, with whom he had one daughter; he remarried on 27 August 1901 to Marie Intermann, with whom he had five children.
- `[4]` He served on Ferntree Gully Shire Council from 1901 to 1935, with four terms as president (1908–09, 1918–19, 1923–24, 1934–35).
- `[5]` In 1919 he was elected to the Victorian Legislative Council as a Nationalist, representing South Eastern Province.
- `[6]` He was Minister of Public Works and Mines from 1928 to 1929 and a minister without portfolio from 1932 to 1935.
- `[7]` Chandler died in Boronia in 1935 and was succeeded by his son Gilbert.

### Gilbert Chandler

- `[0]` Sir Gilbert Lawrence Chandler KBE, CMG (29 August 1903 – 8 April 1974) was a Liberal Party politician who served in the Bolte Ministry in Victoria.
- `[1]` Chandler, a horticulturist, was educated at Scotch College in Melbourne.
- `[2]` As a 25-year-old, Chandler played a game for the Hawthorn Football Club in the 1928 VFL season.
- `[3]` He became a partner in his family's nursery at The Basin in Bayswater before following his father, Alfred, into politics.
- `[4]` **[SF]** When Alfred Chandler died in 1935, Gilbert won the subsequent by-election and took his place as the United Australia Party representative for Southern Province in the Victorian Legislative Council.
- `[5]` In 1935, Chandler also joined the Fern Tree Gully Shire Council, and served as its president in 1938 and 1939.
- `[6]` He switched to the Liberal Party in 1943.
- `[7]` He was minister without portfolio from 1943 to 1945 and as the chairman of the Bush Fire Relief committee from 1944 until 1946.
- `[8]` When Henry Bolte became premier in 1955, he wanted Chandler as the Education Minister, but due to Chandler's horticultural background, Chandler requested to be Minister of Agriculture.
- `[9]` The son-in-law of former politician Jabez Coon, he served in that position until he retired from parliament in 1973.
- `[10]` Chandler was the Minister of State Development, Decentralisation and Immigration briefly in 1956.
- `[11]` In 1962, he was appointed as the leader of the Legislative Council, having been deputy-leader since 1955.
- `[12]` Due to his sporting history, Chandler was a member of the 1956 Summer Olympics organising committee.
- `[13]` He later served as chairman of the Melbourne Cricket Ground in 1973 and 1974.
- `[14]` He died of a coronary occlusion in 1974, at the William Angliss Hospital, which he had been co-founder and president of since 1939.
- `[15]` An agricultural college called the Gilbert Chandler Institute of Dairy Technology at Werribee was named after him.

- `[0]` The place of birth( POB) or birthplace is the place where a person was born.
- `[1]` This place is often used in legal documents, together with name and date of birth, to uniquely identify a person.
- `[2]` As a general rule with respect to passports, the place of birth is determined to be the country that currently has" sovereignty" over the actual place of birth, regardless of when the birth actually occurred.
- `[3]` The place of birth is not necessarily the place where the parents of the new baby live.
- `[4]` If the baby is born in a hospital in another place, that place is the place of birth.
- `[5]` In many countries, this also means that the government requires that the birth of the new baby is registered in the place of birth.
- `[6]` Some countries place less or no importance on the place of birth, instead using alternative geographical characteristics for the purpose of identity documents.
- `[7]` For example, Sweden has used the concept of" födelsehemortdomicile of birth") since 1947.
- `[8]` This means that the domicile of the baby's mother is the registered place of birth.
- `[9]` The location of the maternity ward or other physical birthplace is considered unimportant.
- `[10]` Similarly, Switzerland uses the concept of" Heimatortlieu d'origineluogo d'origineplace of origin" in German, French and Italian respectively).
- `[11]` A child born to Swiss parents is automatically assigned the place of origin of the parent with the same last name, so the child either gets their mother's or father's place of origin.
- `[12]` A child born to one Swiss parent and one foreign parent acquires the place of origin of their Swiss parent.
- `[13]` In a Swiss passport and identity card, the holder's place of origin is stated, not their place of birth.
- `[14]` In some countries( primarily in the Americas), the place of birth automatically determines the nationality of the baby, a practice often referred to by the Latin phrase" jus soli".
- `[15]` Almost all countries outside the Americas instead attribute nationality based on the nationality( ies) of the baby's parents( referred to as" jus sanguinis").
- `[16]` There can be some confusion regarding the place of birth if the birth takes place in an unusual way: when babies are born on an airplane or at sea, difficulties can arise.
- `[17]` The place of birth of such a person depends on the law of the countries involved, which include the nationality of the plane or ship, the nationality( ies) of the parents and/ or the location of the plane or ship( if the birth occurs in the territorial waters or airspace of a country).
- `[18]` Some administrative forms may request the applicant's" country of birth".
- `[19]` It is important to determine from the requester whether the information requested refers to the applicant's" place of birth" or" nationality at birth".
- `[20]` For US citizens born abroad who under the US Constitution acquire US citizenship at the time of birth, the nationality at birth will be USA( American), while the place of birth would be the country in which the actual birth takes place.

### Joseph Wenzel I, Prince of Liechtenstein

- `[0]` Joseph Wenzel I( Josef Wenzel Lorenz; 9 August 1696 – 10 February 1772) was the Prince of Liechtenstein between 1712 and 1718, 1732 and 1745, and 1748 and 1772.
- `[1]` Born at Prague, Joseph Wenzel was the eldest son of Prince Philip Erasmus of Liechtenstein( 11 September 1664 – 13 January 1704) and Countess Christina Theresa von Löwenstein- Wertheim- Rochefort( 12 October 1665 – 14 April 1730).
- `[2]` He was a nephew of Anton Florian.
- `[3]` Joseph was primarily a general and was a very successful campaigner.
- `[4]` In 1745, he was made Generalissimo in Italy and was victorious the following year at the Battle of Piacenza.
- `[5]` In 1753 he was made General Chief Commander in Hungary.
- `[6]` In one of the greatest achievements of his career, he reorganised the Habsburg artillery, partially financed out of his own pocket.
- `[7]` From 1735 to 1736, he was Imperial Envoy to Berlin and he was Ambassador to Paris between 1738 and 1741.
- `[8]` In 1760, he escorted the future bride of Josef II to Vienna.
- `[9]` Joseph managed to rule Liechtenstein three times.
- `[10]` Firstly, in his own right, from 1712 – 1718.
- `[11]` The second time, he ruled as the guardian of Josef Johann Adam between 1732 – 1745 and the third time as representative of the House of Liechtenstein from 1748 – 1772.
- `[12]` He was the 698th Knight of the Order of the Golden Fleece in Austria.
- `[13]` When he died in 1772 in Vienna, without surviving issue, Liechtenstein went to his nephew Franz Joseph I, Prince of Liechtenstein.

### Prince Joseph Wenzel of Liechtenstein

- `[1]` He is second in the line of succession to the Liechtensteiner throne through his father.
